This 5-minute-per-call delay is designed to outlast automated sandboxes, which typically give samples 2-5 minutes of execution time. One sandbox vendor recorded an 8-minute total wait before any malicious behavior appeared.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eKilling your antivirus before it can fight back\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most operationally significant component is the WDAC Code Integrity policy.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWindows Defender Application Control (WDAC) policies tell the operating system which executables are allowed to run. The attackers deploy a deny-list policy to \u003ccode\u003e%WINDIR%\\System32\\CodeIntegrity\\SIPolicy.p7b\u003c/code\u003e that blocks 50 executable paths across six security vendors plus core Windows security services:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003eTarget\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eBlocked executables\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAvast\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAvastSvc.exe, AvastUI.exe, afwServ.exe, aswEngSrv.exe, aswidsagent.exe, aswToolsSvc.exe, wsc_proxy.exe, Icarus components, Overseer\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAVG\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAll executables under Program Files\\AVG\\Antivirus\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAvira\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSecurity, VPN, Fallback Updater, Optimizer Host, Endpoint Protection SDK\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWindows Defender\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMsMpEng.exe, MpCmdRun.exe, MpDefenderCoreService.exe\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWindows Security\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSecurityHealthHost.exe, SecurityHealthService.exe, SecurityHealthSystray.exe\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWindows Update\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMoUsoCoreWorker.exe, wuauclt.exe, wusa.exe\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOthers\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eHitmanPro, Kaspersky Virus Removal Tool, Farbar Recovery Scan Tool\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter the next reboot, none of these executables can start. The AV service is effectively dead. It won't run, it can't scan, and Windows Update can't push signature updates. One victim in the Reddit thread reported that Windows Defender disappeared entirely after running the trojanized installer.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is a known but uncommon TTP (MITRE T1562.001). It requires the installer to run elevated, which the SmartScreen bypass dialog would have prompted for. Users who clicked through both warnings — SmartScreen and UAC — gave the malware everything it needed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow the dropper provisions the bot\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eStage 2's strings are all XOR-obfuscated with the same \u003ccode\u003efywo\u003c/code\u003e key used for its resources. After deobfuscation, we recovered 122 strings including registry paths, API names, and encrypted configuration values.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe dropper writes the bot's initial configuration to \u003ccode\u003eHKCU\\SOFTWARE\\Python\u003c/code\u003e. Both the registry value names and data are RC4-encrypted and hex-encoded — making the registry entries look like random hex strings to casual inspection. We recovered two configuration values:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e15e9100cccadef17d91598e244b0b038\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCampaignID\u003c/strong\u003e a unique identifier for this campaign, used during bot registration with the C2 server.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7a2666057614636498fba93e483028cf\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDDRList\u003c/strong\u003e six dead-drop resolver URLs that the bot uses to discover its C2 servers (detailed in the next section).\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eNotably, the dropper does not write a C2 server list directly. The bot discovers its command-and-control addresses exclusively through the dead-drop resolver mechanism — a design choice that makes the infrastructure resilient to takedowns.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eDead-drop resolvers: how the bot finds its C2\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eA dead-drop resolver (DDR) is an intermediary, a legitimate web page where the operator publishes encrypted C2 addresses. The bot fetches the page, extracts and decrypts the content, and connects to whatever C2 servers it finds. If the operator needs to rotate infrastructure, they update the DDR page and every infected machine follows automatically.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe bot reads its DDR list from the registry, fetches each URL, and RC4-decrypts the page content. Valid payloads start with the prefix \u003ccode\u003eVAL:\u003c/code\u003e followed by a semicolon-separated list of C2 URLs.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe extracted all six dead-drop resolver URLs from the dropper and probed them:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003eURL\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eStatus\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eDecrypted content\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003etelegra[.]ph/tr02-05-02\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eC2 list: parkspringshotel[.]com + auraguest[.]lk\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003etelegra[.]ph/ki06-05-02\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eVAL:XX\u003c/code\u003e — placeholder for future C2 rotation\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003erentry[.]co/zzk7opdu\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSame C2 list\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003erentry[.]org/wayc2qqz\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eVAL:XX\u003c/code\u003e — placeholder\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecodeberg[.]org/etienne74/guix/...\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e404\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRemoved or never created\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e`2wxjoz6lkdxkniksujbdknkugweemx7uvxdr4ejgyipce4tnvktvbvqd.onion/t`\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eNot probed\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eTor hidden service DDR\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe operator has two live C2 servers and four reserve dead-drop pages — two stubs ready for C2 rotation and one Tor fallback. If the current C2 infrastructure gets burned, the operator updates a Telegraph or Rentry page and every infected machine picks up the new address on its next poll cycle.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBeyond the dead-drop mechanism, the bot has a domain generation algorithm (DGA) as a last-resort fallback. It combines four seeds (\u003ccode\u003eXNerWs4I\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003ee55gegnW\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eRjWN0Y9m\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eAemOU1co\u003c/code\u003e) with 14 TLDs and an ISO-week-based rotation to produce 56 candidate domains per week. We probed all current DGA domains — none are registered. The operator is running purely on dead-drop infrastructure for now, but the DGA ensures the botnet can survive even if all resolver pages and C2 servers are taken down simultaneously.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe C2 protocol: RSA-OAEP wrapping AES-GCM\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe reversed the bot's full communication protocol from the PyArmor-protected bytecode. It uses a layered encryption scheme designed to prevent passive interception and session tampering:\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=SAUeCTUe 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=mYiY5fc- 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":967},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=M23-fx2A 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=D03z7_6R 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":735},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=J_oCQFhq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=f2a4U07U 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":570}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=J_oCQFhq","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe bot wraps a fresh AES-256 key with the hardcoded RSA-2048 public key (OAEP, SHA-256) and sends it to the C2. The server proves possession of the private key by returning \u003ccode\u003e\"ok\"\u003c/code\u003e encrypted with AES-GCM. From that point, every message in both directions is AES-256-GCM encrypted with a per-direction sequence counter as authenticated associated data — preventing replay and reordering.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter the handshake, the bot registers with the campaign ID provisioned by the dropper. The server returns a \u003ccode\u003ebot_id\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003ebot_pass\u003c/code\u003e that the bot stores in the registry for subsequent polling. The bot's primary capability is arbitrary Python code execution — the attacker can ship any Python code and the bot will run it and return the output.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAll identified C2 and staging servers are compromised \u003ca href=\"https://qloapps.com/\"\u003eQloApps\u003c/a\u003e hotel reservation websites:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003eDomain\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eRole\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eIP\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003ePHP\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eLocation\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eparkspringshotel[.]com\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eC2 panel\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e172.96.172[.]91\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e8.3.30\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eGhana\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eauraguest[.]lk\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eC2 panel\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e209.133.215[.]178\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e8.3.30\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSri Lanka\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003echeckinnhotels[.]com\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLinux payload staging\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e66.29.137[.]25\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e7.4.33\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e—\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe two C2 servers share identical JARM fingerprints and run the same PHP version, confirming the same C2 panel software is deployed on both. The third domain, \u003ccode\u003echeckinnhotels[.]com\u003c/code\u003e, was used to host the Linux payload disguised as an SVG file (\u003ca href=\"https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/jdownloader-site-hacked-to-replace-installers-with-python-rat-malware/\"\u003eBleepingComputer\u003c/a\u003e). All three run QloApps — an open-source hotel booking CMS — suggesting the attacker specifically targets this software to build their infrastructure. None of the domains had malicious detections on VirusTotal at the time of discovery. We confirmed both C2 servers remain operationally live as of May 11.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe r77 rootkit stager\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe rootkit stager is a 176 KB native x86 PE based on the open-source \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/bytecode77/r77-rootkit\"\u003er77 rootkit\u003c/a\u003e. It contains an embedded .NET assembly that performs four operations:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9ca3772c2bd6052d86931268e23d036a\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAMSI bypass\u003c/strong\u003e — a PowerShell cradle patches \u003ccode\u003eAmsiScanBuffer\u003c/code\u003e in memory, disabling Windows' Antimalware Scan Interface before any script payload runs.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e01c27cd4c721b2dabe18b819d05c84fe\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eReflective DLL injection\u003c/strong\u003e — injects the r77 hooking DLLs (32-bit and 64-bit variants) into \u003ccode\u003ewinlogon.exe\u003c/code\u003e. These DLLs hook Windows API functions to hide any process, file, or registry key whose name starts with the \u003ccode\u003e$77\u003c/code\u003e prefix.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e254d7da7ebd6dfd7d57f7c5f67760ba3\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eRegistry persistence\u003c/strong\u003e — stores the stager assembly as a binary blob in \u003ccode\u003eHKLM\\SOFTWARE\\$77stager\u003c/code\u003e and reloads it at every boot via .NET reflection.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef200c38559748bd535edaf6a0d5aa3d5\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eService creation\u003c/strong\u003e — installs a Windows service (\u003ccode\u003e$77svc\u003c/code\u003e) to ensure the rootkit survives reboots independently of the registry mechanism.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eOnce r77 is active, the \u003ccode\u003e$77\u003c/code\u003e prefix acts as an invisibility cloak. The Python bot calls \u003ccode\u003er77_hide_self()\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003er77_hide_registry_path()\u003c/code\u003e to rename its own process and registry hive with this prefix, making them invisible to Task Manager, \u003ccode\u003eregedit\u003c/code\u003e, and most security tools.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe Python bot under PyArmor v9\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe final payload is a Python script protected by \u003ca href=\"https://pyarmor.dashingsoft.com/\"\u003ePyArmor\u003c/a\u003e v9, a commercial bytecode obfuscation tool. PyArmor encrypts each function's bytecode individually and decrypts it at runtime through a native DLL — making static analysis significantly harder than standard Python malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe built a custom tool to decrypt and disassemble the protected bytecode, recovering 30 functions totaling 7,326 lines of disassembly. The bot is cross-platform (Windows and Linux), uses gevent for asynchronous I/O, and handles the full lifecycle: configuration management, C2 discovery via dead-drop resolvers and DGA, encrypted communication, task execution, and rootkit integration.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe bot's primary capability is executing arbitrary Python code received from the C2 server. Combined with the Python 3.14 runtime installed by the dropper, this gives the operator the ability to deploy any follow-on payload — keyloggers, credential stealers, lateral movement tools — without needing to push new binaries to the victim.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eImpact and recommendations\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eUsers who downloaded JDownloader from the official website between May 6 and May 7, 2026, using the \"Download Alternative Installer\" links (Windows) or the Linux shell installer link are potentially compromised. The \u003ca href=\"https://jdownloader.org/incident_8.5.2026.html\"\u003eofficial incident report\u003c/a\u003e lists eight malicious installer hashes across x86/amd64 and multiple JRE versions. The attackers exploited an unpatched CMS vulnerability to swap download links — the actual installer files hosted externally were never modified. In-app updates, macOS downloads, and package manager installs (Flatpak, Winget, Snap) were not affected.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Linux attack chain, [first reported by BleepingComputer](https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/jdownloader-site-hacked-to-replace-installers-with-python-rat-malware/), follows a different delivery path. We downloaded the trojanized shell installer and its payload to verify the claims and dig deeper.d. The modified install4j script requires root, checks for x86_64 architecture, and downloads a 20 MB tar.gz from \u003ccode\u003echeckinnhotels[.]com/img/logo1.svg\u003c/code\u003e (disguised with \u003ccode\u003eContent-Type: image/svg+xml\u003c/code\u003e — still live as of May 11). The archive contains two files: a 3 KB SUID-root helper (\u003ccode\u003esystemd-exec\u003c/code\u003e) and a 21 MB PyInstaller bundle (\u003ccode\u003epkg\u003c/code\u003e). The helper is a minimal ELF that calls \u003ccode\u003esetuid(0)\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003esetgid(0)\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003eexecvp()\u003c/code\u003e — installed to \u003ccode\u003e/usr/bin/systemd-exec\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003echmod 4755\u003c/code\u003e. Persistence is created via \u003ccode\u003e/etc/profile.d/systemd.sh\u003c/code\u003e, which launches the payload masquerading as \u003ccode\u003e/usr/libexec/upowerd\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe decrypted the PyArmor-protected Python bytecode from the Linux payload and confirmed it is \u003cstrong\u003ethe same bot source code\u003c/strong\u003e as the Windows variant — identical RSA public key, DGA seeds, RC4 keys, C2 protocol, and DDR handler logic. The same campaign ID (\u003ccode\u003eNdcHsJPM\u003c/code\u003e) and all six DDR URLs are written to \u003ccode\u003e~/.local/share/\u003c/code\u003e as RC4-encrypted files (mirroring the Windows registry approach). The only difference is the Python runtime: the Linux bundle uses Python 3.12 (packaged via PyInstaller) versus Python 3.14 on Windows.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe JDownloader developers restored clean downloads on May 9 and published a detailed incident timeline. Users who already had JDownloader installed and received updates through the application's built-in updater were not affected — only fresh downloads from the website during the compromise window were trojanized.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIf you ran the trojanized installer:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e78c8637f87f47adb2a6c0e9e37ff1466\"\u003eThe WDAC policy may have disabled your antivirus. Check \u003ccode\u003e%WINDIR%\\System32\\CodeIntegrity\\SIPolicy.p7b\u003c/code\u003e — if it exists and you didn't create it, delete it and reboot.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5a9344602452a9e77dfe81e30c45fd8c\"\u003eThe registry keys \u003ccode\u003eHKCU\\SOFTWARE\\Python\u003c/code\u003e (bot config) and \u003ccode\u003eHKLM\\SOFTWARE\\$77stager\u003c/code\u003e (rootkit) are strong indicators of compromise — but if r77 is active, its API hooks hide all \u003ccode\u003e$77\u003c/code\u003e-prefixed registry keys and the bot's own hive from standard tools like \u003ccode\u003eregedit\u003c/code\u003e. To inspect these, boot from a Windows recovery environment or mount the registry hives offline.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"edcbc2f271c7b2885893f8f995fe252eb\"\u003eA clean OS reinstall is the safest remediation. The r77 rootkit's reflective injection into \u003ccode\u003ewinlogon.exe\u003c/code\u003e and AMSI bypass make partial cleanup unreliable.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFor defenders:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"efcd7ce434d65f159da0dbff097d5fb44\"\u003eThe bot uses the default Python User-Agent \u003ccode\u003ePython-urllib/3.14\u003c/code\u003e. While not unique on its own, it can help correlate connections to the C2 URLs and DDR pages listed below.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e35fe4757c01a79131defbd71e6762bfb\"\u003eMonitor for DNS queries to the DGA pattern: 15-character lowercase alphanumeric hostnames on TLDs including \u003ccode\u003e.top\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.cfd\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.sbs\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.cyou\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.icu\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.casa\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e41ab2df0dd210b9e338012701bdf4398\"\u003eThe mutex name \u003ccode\u003eGlobal\\0C3C1D37\u003c/code\u003e can serve as a host-based indicator.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e68e99d7961368cd02568989f8e767d08\"\u003eBlock the dead-drop resolver pages: \u003ccode\u003etelegra[.]ph/tr02-05-02\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003etelegra[.]ph/ki06-05-02\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003erentry[.]co/zzk7opdu\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003erentry[.]org/wayc2qqz\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of Compromise\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFull IOC Cryptography\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eT1573.002\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRootkit\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eT1014\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCreate Registry\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eT1112\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor customers using Norton, Avast, AVG, Avira or any other security product, the most important question is whether the malicious installer was executed. If you downloaded an affected installer but did not run it, delete the file, download a fresh installer from the official JDownloader site, and run a full scan with up-to-date protection. If you ran the installer, or cannot rule out that you ran it, treat the system as compromised. JDownloader recommends a clean operating system reinstall in this situation, and we agree that this is the safest baseline. The Windows payload includes rootkit behavior, persistence, encrypted configuration and policy-based defense evasion, so a normal scan may reduce risk but cannot guarantee that every persistence mechanism has been removed. Until the system has been rebuilt or inspected from a trusted recovery environment, avoid using it for sensitive activity. Do not log in to email, banking, work accounts, password managers or other important services from that machine. Change important passwords from another trusted device, and monitor accounts for unusual 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":120,"height":120}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=uIL3f30w"}}],"contributingAuthors":[],"products":[],"paragraphs":[{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eTLDR: \u003c/strong\u003eBedep was a malware family active mainly in 2014 and 2015 that used an unusually sophisticated domain generation algorithm, or DGA, to hide its command-and-control infrastructure. Instead of relying only on the date, it used real foreign exchange rates published by the \u003cem\u003eEuropean Central Bank\u003c/em\u003e, making its future domains much harder to predict in advance. Under the hood, the malware used ideas from group theory to generate a fixed set of unique domains without collisions, a rare example of advanced mathematics being applied directly in real-world malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost domain generation algorithms are boring. A linear congruential generator, a date seed, some modular arithmetic, a character table -- \u003ccode\u003eseed = (seed * 0x41C64E6D + 0x3039) % 2^31\u003c/code\u003e, rinse, repeat. You reverse them in an afternoon, pre-compute next month's domains over lunch, and move on.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBedep author implemented a number theory textbook inside a 32-bit DLL, and the result is one of the most mathematically elegant pieces of malware ever shipped.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis post is a deep dive into this unique engineering.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat is Bedep?\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eBedep is an ad-fraud botnet that was active from late 2014 through 2015, delivered exclusively through the Angler exploit kit. When a Flash zero-day (CVE-2015-0311) was burning, Angler was dropping Bedep. The malware itself was a modular downloader focused on click fraud, but its infrastructure is what made it interesting. In early 2015, new variants appeared with a DGA for C2 resolution. A three-day sinkhole by Arbor Networks (ASERT) caught phone-homes from roughly 82,000 unique IPs, spread across every continent except -- notably -- Russia.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDennis Schwarz originally reversed the DGA at ASERT, who published a proof-of-concept Python reimplementation and a detailed write-up titled \u003cem\u003e\"Bedep's DGA: Trading Foreign Exchange for Malware Domains.\"\u003c/em\u003e The original blog post has since been taken down from NETSCOUT's site, but survives on the \u003ca href=\"https://web.archive.org/web/20160311083109/http://www.arbornetworks.com:80/blog/asert/bedeps-dga-trading-foreign-exchange-for-malware-domains/\"\u003eWayback Machine\u003c/a\u003e. The PoC lived at \u003ccode\u003egithub.com/arbor/bedep_dga\u003c/code\u003e. Dennis got the algorithm working but noted that parts of it were opaque -- he called the core transform a \"blackbox\" and flagged the embedded table as \"likely something Fermat number related.\" He was right. Let's open that box.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe ECB trick: Seeding a DGA from the financial markets\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eBefore the math starts, Bedep needs a seed that both the bot and the botmaster can independently derive without communicating. Most DGAs use the date. Bedep uses the date \u003cem\u003eand\u003c/em\u003e the global foreign exchange market.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe algorithm fetches two XML files from legitimate public services:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec5890edf5187711796f804fae7ad8577\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUTC time\u003c/strong\u003e from \u003ccode\u003eearthtools.org/timezone/0/0\u003c/code\u003e -- the current timestamp, converted to \"days since year zero.\"\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ead6313dc967de96442575ff50339b412\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eEuro foreign exchange reference rates\u003c/strong\u003e from \u003ccode\u003ewww.ecb.europa.eu/stats/eurofxref/eurofxref-hist-90d.xml\u003c/code\u003e -- the European Central Bank's daily publication of EUR conversion rates against 31+ world currencies, updated every business day.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe time is used to select which day's rates to use. Specifically, Bedep picks \"last Tuesday's\" exchange rates -- meaning the preceding week's Tuesday until Thursday, after which it rolls forward to the current week's Tuesday. Only rates falling on a Monday boundary (in the algorithm's day-counting scheme) are considered. This gives a roughly weekly rotation with a deterministic selection rule.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom the chosen date, Bedep extracts up to 48 currency rates -- USD, JPY, GBP, CZK, BGN, HUF, and so on. Each rate is parsed through a slightly broken custom \u003ccode\u003eatof()\u003c/code\u003e implementation (off by a bit in the least significant digits -- a quirk, not a feature), packed into a 64-bit IEEE double, and its low 32-bit dword is extracted for use in the algorithm.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHere is what a single run looks like, from the PoC output for April 7, 2015:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eparsed 31 currencies from 2015-04-07 (currency date): \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;USD: 1.0847 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;JPY: 130.33 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; BGN: 1.9558 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;CZK: 27.455 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;DKK: 7.4714 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;GBP: 0.7286 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;HUF: 299.08 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;PLN: 4.0578 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;RON: 4.4165 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;SEK: 9.374 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; CHF: 1.0438 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;NOK: 8.73 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;HRK: 7.619 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; RUB: 59.8265 \u0026nbsp; TRY: 2.8079 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;AUD: 1.4192 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;BRL: 3.3979 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;CAD: 1.3563 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;CNY: 6.7241 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;HKD: 8.4086 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;IDR: 14091.77 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;ILS: 4.267 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; INR: 67.598 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;KRW: 1183.28 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;MXN: 16.1919 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; MYR: 3.9527 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;NZD: 1.4423 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;PHP: 48.3 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;SGD: 1.4724 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;THB: 35.335 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;ZAR: 12.8345\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eWhy is this clever? Because the ECB rates are:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5f3c952abb440c715bcfa626ac9bd4c0\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePublicly available\u003c/strong\u003e -- anyone can fetch the same XML\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7730a0d72c71be00a0c3d3feebc10618\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eGlobally consistent\u003c/strong\u003e -- every bot on every continent gets the same data\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1667d4c4edd48ffbda05118e24153bf0\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUnpredictable in advance\u003c/strong\u003e -- nobody can predict next Tuesday's EUR/USD rate\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e80b9125dab13aae37cb9ed8b88656475\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHistorically verifiable\u003c/strong\u003e -- the ECB publishes archives going back years\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0d9bf0abba3c6cb7703705ce6203f707\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eImpossible to tamper with\u003c/strong\u003e -- the ECB is not going to modify their reference rates to help you sinkhole a botnet\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd because the malware fetches this from \u003ccode\u003eecb.europa.eu\u003c/code\u003e, the request looks like legitimate traffic. A Snort signature (SID 33188) was written to detect it, but it fired constantly on real users checking exchange rates.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe mathematical core\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is where Bedep stops being a clever botnet and starts being a discrete mathematics exercise.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003ePrecomputed subgroup parameters\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe DLL embeds a lookup table -- 102 entries of 8 dwords each, stored as JSON in the PoC (\u003ccode\u003etransform2_table_varN.json\u003c/code\u003e, one per config variant). At first glance it looks like random constants. It is not.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003etransform2\u003c/code\u003e function takes a hash derived from the exchange rates and days-since value, then uses it as an index into this table. Each table entry, after being decoded through config-specific XOR and multiply constants (\u003ccode\u003e0x663d81 * entry[0] ^ value3\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003e0x281 * entry[1] ^ value2\u003c/code\u003e), yields two critical values:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee4084ac1a9ee922a9a2585119d02acb9\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ep\u003c/strong\u003e -- a prime number\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7d298adce011a8fe31f7d5db7f1685ee\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eq\u003c/strong\u003e -- an integer such that q divides p - 1\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the subgroup structure. The table stores primes whose group orders (p - 1) have known factorizations with small prime factors. The malware doesn't need to factor p - 1 at runtime -- that hard work was done offline by the author and baked into the DLL. Dennis Schwarz noted these were \"likely something Fermat number related,\" and he was onto something: the primes are chosen so that their totient has a smooth factorization, making generator-finding tractable.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe number of domains to generate comes directly from q - 1. For the first config, this works out to 22 domains; for the second, 28 -- totaling 50 domains per weekly rotation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eFinding generators: A textbook primitive root search\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith p and the prime factorization of p - 1 in hand, Bedep needs a generator of the multiplicative group ((\\mathbb{Z}/p\\mathbb{Z})^*) -- or more precisely, of a specific subgroup of order q.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003etransform8\u003c/code\u003e function does this in two stages. First, it runs a \u003cstrong\u003etrial-division prime sieve\u003c/strong\u003e: starting from 3, stepping by 2 (skipping evens), it tests each candidate against all previously found primes. It collects up to 37 small primes, then filters for those that actually divide q (the subgroup order). This gives the prime factorization of q.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThen \u003ccode\u003etransform3\u003c/code\u003e finds the \u003cstrong\u003eprimitive root\u003c/strong\u003e itself. This is the standard algorithm straight from a number theory textbook:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003edef find_primitive_root(p, prime_factors_of_order): \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;for g in range(2, p // 2): \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;is_generator = True \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;for q_i in prime_factors_of_order: \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# If g^((p-1)/q_i) ≡ 1 (mod p), then g generates \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# a proper subgroup, not the full group. Reject it. \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;if pow(g, (p - 1) // q_i, p) == 1: \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;is_generator = False \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;break \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;if is_generator: \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;return g \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;return None\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe actual code in \u003ccode\u003etransform3\u003c/code\u003e is this logic rendered in C-style loop structure with manual index tracking, but the mathematics is identical. For each candidate g starting from 2, it checks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e[ g^{(p-1)/q_i} \\not\\equiv 1 \\pmod{p} \\quad \\text{for all prime factors } q_i \\text{ of } p-1 ]\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf g passes all checks, it generates the full group. This is literally the algorithm from Section 11.1 of Shoup's \u003cem\u003eA Computational Introduction to Number Theory and Algebra\u003c/em\u003e, implemented in a malware DLL.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWalking the cyclic group\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eNow comes the payoff. With a prime p, a generator g, and a subgroup order q, the \u003ccode\u003etransform7\u003c/code\u003e function sets up the DGA's iteration state:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003emodulus \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;= p \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# field_c seed \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; = pow(g, random_e, p) \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# field_4 -- starting element step \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; = pow(g2, random_f, q) \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; # field_8 -- step exponent\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe starting position \u003ccode\u003eseed\u003c/code\u003e is g raised to a random exponent (derived from \u003ccode\u003erdtsc\u003c/code\u003e, the CPU timestamp counter). The step is similarly randomized from a second generator. Both are computed via modular exponentiation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThen, for each domain, the iteration is simply:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eseed = pow(seed, step, modulus) \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; # one step through the cyclic group domain = seed_to_domain(seed, currencies)\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThat single line -- \u003ccode\u003epow(seed, step, modulus)\u003c/code\u003e -- is the entire DGA engine. Each call advances the state to the next element of the cyclic subgroup.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBecause the subgroup has order q, and step is coprime to q (guaranteed by the generator construction), this walk visits every non-identity element of the subgroup exactly once before cycling. The number of domains generated equals q - 1, which is precisely the number of non-identity elements. No collisions. No wasted iterations. No off-by-one hoping you hit enough domains.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe key insight:\u003c/strong\u003e the set of group elements visited is deterministic (fixed by p, g, and the exchange rates), but the \u003cem\u003eorder\u003c/em\u003e of visitation depends on the random exponents from \u003ccode\u003erdtsc\u003c/code\u003e. Every infected machine walks through the same set of q - 1 elements, generating the same q - 1 domain names, but potentially in a different order. Same destinations, different paths.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eflowchart TB \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;subgraph cyclic_group [\"Cyclic Subgroup of (Z/pZ)*\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E1[\"g^1 mod p\"] --\u0026gt; E2[\"g^2 mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E2 --\u0026gt; E3[\"g^3 mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E3 --\u0026gt; E4[\"...\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E4 --\u0026gt; Eq[\"g^(q-1) mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;Eq --\u0026gt; E1 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;end \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;subgraph walk [\"DGA Walk (order depends on rdtsc)\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S1[\"seed_0\"] --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| S2[\"seed_1\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S2 --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| S3[\"seed_2\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S3 --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| S4[\"...\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S4 --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| SN[\"seed_(q-2)\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;end \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S1 -.-\u0026gt;|\"= g^e mod p\\n(random start)\"| cyclic_group \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S2 -.-\u0026gt;|maps to| E3 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S3 -.-\u0026gt;|maps to| Eq\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eFrom Group Elements to Domain Names\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach group element is a 32-bit integer. Turning it into a domain name is the final step, handled by \u003ccode\u003etransform11\u003c/code\u003e. This is less mathematically elegant and more of a traditional mixing function, but it has a nice property: every character position in the domain uses a \u003cem\u003edifferent\u003c/em\u003e currency rate.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe algorithm cycles through the full list of parsed currencies (31 in the example above). For a domain of length 18, that means character 0 is influenced by USD, character 1 by HRK, character 2 by MXN, character 3 by GBP, and so on. Each character is computed by:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1b4d60fdb8fc1f153baf8a99855a0b35\"\u003eMultiplying the currency's 3-letter name (packed as a 32-bit int) and its 64-bit floating-point rate by large constants\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec05b47f97cd63ed0295b790fd5474c85\"\u003eXORing and shifting the result with the current seed and the days-since value\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e2f1e2c5d769b5e8978ca12f1d05cf791\"\u003eTaking the result modulo 26 (for positions 2+) or modulo 36 (for the last two positions, allowing digits)\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e589b38ae5aa632fba755388fc26bd71c\"\u003eAdding the ASCII offset for 'a'\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe domain length itself is derived from the seed XORed with a rate-dependent value, bounded between 12 and 18 characters. All domains use the \u003ccode\u003e.com\u003c/code\u003e TLD.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA trace from the PoC output shows the mixing in action:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eseed: 0xa13c9652 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;ECB[\"ECB XML\\n31+ currency rates\"] --\u0026gt; DateSelect[\"Select last\\nTuesday's rates\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;Earth[\"earthtools.org\\nUTC timestamp\"] --\u0026gt; DateSelect \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;DateSelect --\u0026gt; Mix[\"XOR/multiply\\nrates + days_since\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;Mix --\u0026gt; TableLookup[\"Lookup table\\n→ prime p, order q\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;TableLookup --\u0026gt; GenFind[\"Sieve primes,\\nfind generator g\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;GenFind --\u0026gt; GroupSetup[\"seed = g^rand mod p\\nstep = g2^rand mod q\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;GroupSetup --\u0026gt; Walk[\"Iterate:\\nseed = seed^step mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; Each malware variant embedded two configs that ran per rotation -- the first generating 22 domains, the second 28, yielding 50 domains total. Across the observed campaign, domains were registered 2-5 days ahead of use through \"Domain Context\" registrar with Regway nameservers, all resolving to infrastructure at 5.196.181.244 and 46.105.251.1.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy this matters\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCompare Bedep's approach with its contemporaries:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003eFamily\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eDGA Technique\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eSeed\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eConficker.C\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDate + simple arithmetic\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCurrent date\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eGamaredon\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eNested character-range loops\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eHardcoded ranges\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eNecurs\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCRC32 hash of date components\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDate + constant\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMatsnu\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDictionary word concatenation\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDays since epoch\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBedep\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCyclic group walk mod p\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eECB exchange rates\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eEvery other DGA on this list can be pre-computed arbitrarily far into the future. Bedep cannot -- because Tuesday's exchange rates don't exist until Tuesday. You can sinkhole Conficker's domains for next year during your coffee break. For Bedep, you have to wait for the ECB to publish, then race the botmaster to register the domains.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe mathematical guarantee is real: the cyclic group structure ensures exactly q - 1 distinct domains per rotation with no collisions and no wasted iterations. The primitive root construction ensures the generator produces every element. The precomputed tables avoid expensive factorization at runtime. The exchange rate seed provides unpredictable entropy from a trusted, globally-consistent, publicly-verifiable source.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is not someone who copy-pasted a DGA from a forum. The author understood multiplicative groups modulo primes, knew how to find primitive roots, precomputed smooth-order primes offline, and wired the whole thing to the European Central Bank's daily publications. Whether they learned this from a university course, a textbook, or a cryptography library's source code, the result is unmistakable: this is applied algebra, deployed in production, at scale.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe original ASERT analysis is preserved on the \u003ca href=\"https://web.archive.org/web/20160311083109/http://www.arbornetworks.com:80/blog/asert/bedeps-dga-trading-foreign-exchange-for-malware-domains/\"\u003eWayback Machine\u003c/a\u003e. The proof-of-concept implementation was published on \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/arbor/bedep_dga\"\u003eASERT's GitHub\u003c/a\u003e. ","authors":[{"socialLinks":[],"bio":"A group of elite researchers who like to stay under the radar.","displayName":"Threat Research Team","title":"Threat Research Team","picture":{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=Y7ujGs-y 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=zua2szJm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=KgS0xjxS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=iDSFMEuk 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":120,"height":120}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=uIL3f30w"}}],"contributingAuthors":[],"products":[],"paragraphs":[{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eIn the spring of 2022, our anti-rootkit engine flagged something unusual inside a svchost.exe process on a machine in the United Kingdom. A chunk of memory -roughly 32 kilobytes- was making direct NtQuerySystemInformation syscalls, bypassing every usermode hook we had in place. When we pulled the memory dump and opened it in a disassembler, we found not just shellcode, but an entire virtual machine: a custom bytecode interpreter running a 25-kilobyte program, decoding its own payload on the fly, resolving APIs through hash lookups, and checking for hypervisors before executing a single line of its real code.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe named it AngrySpark -after the C++ namespace angry_spark found in the RTTI metadata of the DLL that delivered it. Over the following year, we captured three memory snapshots from the same host, watched its C2 infrastructure evolve, and eventually saw the connection get blocked. Then it vanished. No new samples. No new infrastructure. No second victim. Just a single spark in the dark.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the story of how we took it apart.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy AngrySpark matters\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost backdoors pick a lane. AngrySpark built three of them. The DLL, the VM, and the beacon each carry their own encryption, their own API resolution, and their own C2 channel. Compromise one layer and the other two remain opaque.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat architecture is what elevates it from a well-built backdoor to something worth writing up. The payload is assembled from bytecode at runtime, so the operator can swap out the final-stage binary, change the API set, add anti-analysis checks, or rotate C2 URLs by pushing a new 25KB blob. The 6.5KB loader never changes. It behaves more like a deployment framework than a conventional dropper.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe other reason it matters is the mismatch between effort and scope. The engineering is serious: a VM-based deployment framework, dual encrypted C2 channels, CET-aware anti-analysis, runtime code patching, and direct syscalls with a 24-build compatibility table. Yet it appeared on a single machine in the United Kingdom, ran for about a year, and disappeared when its infrastructure expired.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNo public malware repository carried the DLL during its operational life, and no other vendor published on it. That does not make AngrySpark more important than broader campaigns, but it does make it unusually revealing. It looks like high-effort malware deployed with a very limited visible footprint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe architecture at a glance\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark operates as a three-stage system. A DLL masquerading as a Windows component loads via the Task Scheduler, decrypts its configuration from the registry, and injects position-independent shellcode into svchost.exe. That shellcode implements a virtual machine. The VM processes a 25KB blob of bytecode instructions, decoding and assembling the real payload -- a beacon that profiles the machine, phones home over HTTPS disguised as PNG image requests, and can receive encrypted shellcode for execution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach stage is designed to be independently replaceable. The VM's bytecode format means the operator can swap out the payload, change the configuration, rotate C2 URLs, or add new anti-analysis checks -- all without modifying a single byte of the loader code.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eStage\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eRole\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eKey idea\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eStage 0, DLL loader\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMasquerades as a Windows Task Scheduler extension, decrypts config from the registry, and injects shellcode into svchost.exe\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eA quiet entry point with persistence, configuration handling, and self-removal logic\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eStage 1, VM loader\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRuns a custom bytecode interpreter that assembles the real payload at runtime\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eThe operator can change behavior by updating the blob rather than the loader\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eStage 2, beacon\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eProfiles the host, communicates over HTTPS disguised as PNG image requests, and can execute encrypted shellcode from the server\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eA full C2 channel built to blend in and stay adaptable\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStage 0, the DLL loader\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe entry point is a 200KB DLL named WptsExtensions.dll, the exact name of a legitimate Windows Task Scheduler extension library. It exports the same five functions (WptsCopyActionData, WptsCreateAction, WptsDestroyAction, WptsFreeActionData, WptsLaunchAction) and spoofs its version info as \"Microsoft Corporation, v10.0.19041.1023\".\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter loading its configuration, the DLL injects the VM shellcode into a svchost.exe service host process. The injected code is entirely position-independent. It carries its own API resolver, its own exception handler, and its own syscall dispatcher. No imports, no relocations, no PE headers.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStage 1, the virtual machine\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM loader is a ~6.5KB position-independent shellcode that implements a bytecode interpreter. It reads instructions sequentially from a 25KB configuration blob, dispatching each to a handler function. The blob contains approximately 190 entries (VM instructions) encoded in a custom format.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe May 2022 variant has 23 entries (syscall tables). Between May and June 2022, the operator added a single entry: build 20344, a Windows 10 Insider preview from the Iron/Cobalt development cycle -- the branch that became Windows 11. The June and January variants are identical (24 entries, table at 0x13A2).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis tells us two things. First, the operator was actively maintaining the syscall table and testing against Insider builds during May-June 2022. Second, they stopped updating after that. Builds above 22000 (Win11 22H2, 23H2) are not covered, and the entry-point guard rejects them outright. The shellcode was frozen sometime in mid-2022.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eStage 2, the beacon\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe assembled payload is a 27KB position-independent shellcode with 74 functions. It implements a full C2 beacon with system profiling, encrypted communication, and remote code execution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe beacon disguises its check-in as a request for a PNG image:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe encrypted shellcode is decrypted with the second XXTEA key from the configuration, then executed in freshly allocated memory. After execution, the results are XXTEA-encrypted and sent back via HTTP POST to a separate .php endpoint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eStealth, anti-analysis, and self-removal\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark is not only modular, it is also careful about how it appears to defenders. Several design choices look specifically aimed at frustrating clustering, bypassing instrumentation, and limiting the forensic residue left behind.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe binary's PE metadata has been deliberately altered to confuse toolchain fingerprinting.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eNotable defensive friction points\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed531a883985e6ff1ea0b5526cc40abfc\"\u003eThe PE timestamp is falsified, and the Rich header was modified after compilation in a way that breaks straightforward toolchain fingerprinting.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9519384a4b6896277de1f1a911e912fd\"\u003eThe shellcode uses direct syscalls to get around usermode API hooks.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eee6db62c8e4b4429a19949b6ac9524f9\"\u003eThe VM includes hypervisor checks and CET or Shadow Stack aware anti-analysis logic.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0bba18ae1c14f5eb447d8061bc1654bc\"\u003eThe DLL pins the C2 server's TLS certificate fingerprint, rejecting connections to anything that does not match the expected infrastructure. If someone sinkholed the domain or stood up a proxy with a different certificate, the DLL would refuse to talk.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecbf5d179746f6984a71674a8cdd15326\"\u003eThe loader can remove itself if contact goes stale or if the server explicitly tells it to do so.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe heartbeat serves a second purpose: it is a dead man's switch. On each beacon cycle, the DLL compares the current time against the stored heartbeat. If the difference exceeds a configurable threshold (stored as a day count in the config blob, multiplied by 864000000000 -- one day in 100-nanosecond FILETIME intervals), the DLL enters its self-destruct sequence: it wipes the encrypted registry blob via RegDeleteValue, then schedules its own DLL file for deletion on the next reboot using MoveFileExW with the MOVEFILE_DELAY_UNTIL_REBOOT flag. Before that, it impersonates itself with SeSecurityPrivilege, SeRestorePrivilege, and SeTakeOwnershipPrivilege, moves the DLL to a temp directory, and restricts its permissions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is a second trigger. If the C2 server responds with HTTP 200 (rather than the expected 302 redirect that delivers encrypted commands), the DLL treats this as a server-side kill command. It runs the same cleanup: wipe registry, schedule file deletion, exit. The normal operating response is a 302 redirect whose Location header provides the URL for the encrypted payload exchange.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn other words: a 200 means \"acknowledged, self-destruct,\" and a 302 means \"here are your orders.\" No contact at all for too long also means self-destruct. The operator built the backdoor to vanish automatically if it lost its handler -- which is exactly what happened in April 2023.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eCommand and control design, and how the operation evolved\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark uses two separate C2 channels, each with its own domain, IP address, and encryption layer.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe two C2 channels don't share anything except a hosting provider. AES-256-CBC + RSA-4096 on one side, XXTEA + custom Base64 + PNG steganography on the other. Different encryption, different protocols, different domains, different IPs, all within the same 185.151.28.0/22 range. The custom Base64 alphabet rotates with each configuration update, so there's no fixed encoding to write a signature against.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach configuration update carried new randomized URL paths, though the domain and C2 structure remained consistent:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe pattern is clear: random 6-8 character slug, incrementing 4-digit counter, random filename. Combined with a custom Base64 alphabet that also rotates per update, this defeats static network signatures while maintaining a recognizable structure for the operator.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePoint in time\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWhat changed\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMay 2022\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMay 2022: The first variant uses NtQuerySystemInformation(40) (SystemCodeIntegrityInformation). The shellcode prologue differs from later versions. Syscall table has 23 entries, no Insider builds.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJune 2022\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJune 2022: Rewritten loader with a different code structure but identical C2 domain. URL paths rotated. Syscall table gains one entry: build 20344 (Win10 Insider Fe/Co), bumping it to 24 entries. The operator was actively updating both code and compatibility data.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJanuary 2023\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eJanuary 2023: Same code as June 2022 (identical syscall table), but the NtQuerySystemInformation argument changed to class 10 (SystemModuleInformation) and URLs rotated again. The code was frozen; only the configuration blob changed.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eApril to June 2023\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eBy April 2023, the C2 connection was blocked. By May, the beacon domain expired. By June, the DLL's C2 certificate expired. The infrastructure was never renewed.\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe timeline suggests active maintenance in mid-2022, followed by a freeze in the shellcode and continued rotation only in the configuration and network paths. Then the infrastructure stopped being renewed. That sequence matters because it makes the disappearance look operational, not accidental.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat we still do not know\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eYet it appeared on a single machine in the United Kingdom, ran for about a year, and disappeared when its infrastructure expired. The DLL never surfaced in any public malware archive. No other vendor has published on it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWas it a targeted operation that achieved its objective and was burned? A test deployment that was abandoned? A contractor's proof of concept? We don't know.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat we do know is that somewhere in the RTTI metadata of a tampered DLL, someone chose the namespace angry_spark and the exception class sombrely_exception. And populated it with nine C++ classes: BCryptSymmetricKey, BCryptAsymmetricKey, BCryptKey, crypto_exception, connection_exception, wininet_exception, angry_spark_exception, bad_config_exception, and -- curiously -- sombrely_exception. They built a virtual machine to deploy their payload, wrapped it in three layers of encryption, hid their commands inside PNG images, and then -- quietly, sombrely -- let it all expire.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe spark went out. But the code remains.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor readers who want the deeper reverse engineering detail, the appendix below preserves the underlying technical observations, tables, and selected code fragments from the working draft in a more reference-oriented format.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eTechnical Appendix\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAppendix A. Detailed Stage 0 notes\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe entry point is a 200KB DLL named WptsExtensions.dll, the exact name of a legitimate Windows Task Scheduler extension library. It exports the same five functions (WptsCopyActionData, WptsCreateAction, WptsDestroyAction, WptsFreeActionData, WptsLaunchAction) and spoofs its version info as \"Microsoft Corporation, v10.0.19041.1023\".\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe DLL was never uploaded to VirusTotal. It remained invisible to the public threat intelligence ecosystem for the entirety of its operational life.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe DLL stores its encrypted configuration in the Windows registry under a path that itself is encrypted.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA heartbeat timestamp (FILETIME) at bytes 0-7 of the registry value is updated after each successful beacon, allowing the operator to track the last check-in time.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe heartbeat serves a second purpose: it is a dead man's switch. On each beacon cycle, the DLL compares the current time against the stored heartbeat. If the difference exceeds a configurable threshold (stored as a day count in the config blob, multiplied by 864000000000 -- one day in 100-nanosecond FILETIME intervals), the DLL enters its self-destruct sequence: it wipes the encrypted registry blob via RegDeleteValue, then schedules its own DLL file for deletion on the next reboot using MoveFileExW with the MOVEFILE_DELAY_UNTIL_REBOOT flag. Before that, it impersonates itself with SeSecurityPrivilege, SeRestorePrivilege, and SeTakeOwnershipPrivilege, moves the DLL to a temp directory, and restricts its permissions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is a second trigger. If the C2 server responds with HTTP 200 (rather than the expected 302 redirect that delivers encrypted commands), the DLL treats this as a server-side kill command. It runs the same cleanup: wipe registry, schedule file deletion, exit. The normal operating response is a 302 redirect whose Location header provides the URL for the encrypted payload exchange.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn other words: a 200 means \"acknowledged, self-destruct,\" and a 302 means \"here are your orders.\" No contact at all for too long also means self-destruct. The operator built the backdoor to vanish automatically if it lost its handler -- which is exactly what happened in April 2023.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter loading its configuration, the DLL injects the VM shellcode into a svchost.exe service host process. The injected code is entirely position-independent. It carries its own API resolver, its own exception handler, and its own syscall dispatcher. No imports, no relocations, no PE headers.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eClaimed tool Detailed Stage 1 notes\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM loader is a ~6.5KB position-independent shellcode that implements a bytecode interpreter. It reads instructions sequentially from a 25KB configuration blob, dispatching each to a handler function. The blob contains approximately 190 entries encoded in a custom format:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM implements 22 distinct opcodes -- a complete deployment toolkit:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA typical blob is processed in phases:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEvery string, API name, DLL name, and code chunk in the blob is encoded with a cascading XOR cipher. The format is deceptively simple:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e[total_length: 1 byte][data: N bytes][checksum1: 1 byte][checksum2: 1 byte]\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe two checksum bytes double as XOR keys. Decoding works backwards through the data, using separate key chains for even and odd byte positions. After decoding, the original checksums are recomputed using seeds 0x23 and 0xA5 and verified against the saved keys. If either mismatches, the entry is rejected.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe maximum decoded payload is 253 bytes (limited by the single-byte length field). This is why the 27KB payload requires 93 separate DECODE_COPY instructions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM resolves all APIs by walking the Process Environment Block (PEB), iterating loaded modules, and comparing DJB2 hashes of export names:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ehash = 5381\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003efor byte in name:\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ehash = (hash * 33 + byte) \u0026amp; 0xFFFFFFFF\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTwo variants exist: one for UNICODE_STRING (with case folding via | 0x20), used to find DLLs by name, and one for ASCII byte strings, used to match export table entries.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable blob regions\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAnti-analysis\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eANTI_ANALYSIS, SET_FLAGS\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eHypervisor + CET/Shadow Stack detection, selective debug traps\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eInfrastructure\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSETUP_TRAMPOLINE, INIT_EXCEPT, interpretation\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHash\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eTarget\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x22D3B5ED\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003entdll.dll\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x7040EE75\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eKERNEL32.dll\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0xA721952B\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eKERNELBASE.dll\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0xFF6C2F6E\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003emsvcrt.dll\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0xA1CBA7D3\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRtlVirtualUnwind\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0xFA44C769\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRtlLookupFunctionEntry\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo bypass usermode API hooks, the loader includes a syscall instruction wrapper with a hardcoded build-number-to-syscall-number table:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe extracted and compared the syscall table from all three memory dumps. The tables are almost identical, with one revealing difference:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003emov r10, [rsp+38h] ; table entry pointer\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003emovzx eax, word ptr [r10+2] ; syscall number\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003emov r10, rcx ; first argument\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003esyscall\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eret\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable 7 / SP1\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x20\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x20\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x20\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e9200\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWindows 8\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x21\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x21\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x21\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e9600\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWindows 8.1\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x22\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x22\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x22\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e10240-19044\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWin10 1507-21H2\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e20344\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWin10 Insider (Fe/Co)\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e--\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e22000\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eWin11 21H2\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x23\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe May 2022 variant has 23 entries (table at offset 0x1312). Between May and June 2022, the operator added a single entry: build 20344, a Windows 10 Insider preview from the Iron/Cobalt development cycle -- the branch that became Windows 11. The June and January variants are identical (24 entries, table at 0x13A2).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis tells us two things. First, the operator was actively maintaining the syscall table and testing against Insider builds during May-June 2022. Second, they stopped updating after that. Builds above 22000 (Win11 22H2, 23H2) are not covered, and the entry-point guard rejects them outright. The shellcode was frozen sometime in mid-2022.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM scans msvcrt.dll's code sections for a specific 12-byte x64 instruction pattern:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e48 8D 15 XX XX XX XX lea rdx, [rip + offset] ; string ptr\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e48 8B C8 mov rcx, rax\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eFF 15 YY YY YY YY call [rip + offset] ; indirect call\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen a match is found, the VM XOR-decodes a target string, compares it against the data at the lea target, and if it matches, captures the indirect call target -- provided it points into KERNELBASE.dll or KERNEL32.dll. This hijacks specific CRT internal function pointers for exception dispatch, allowing the loader to intercept control flow without modifying the exception handler chain.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBefore any payload code runs, the VM applies several checks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe RDSSPQ instruction reads the Shadow Stack Pointer. Intel's Control-flow Enforcement Technology (CET) is primarily found in instrumented environments and certain hypervisors, so a non-zero SSP is a strong signal that the code is running under analysis or in a hardened VM.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDuring our emulation of the VM bytecode, three instruction types initially defied classification:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eType 21 is the anti-sandbox kill switch. The 4-byte parameter controls it: when set to 0, the VM checks for a hypervisor (via CPUID) and a non-zero Shadow Stack Pointer (via the RDSSPQ instruction). If both are detected on Windows 10 version 2004 or later, the entire loader aborts with error code 0xAACFBB1A. This is a targeted anti-sandbox technique -- most analysis environments running recent Windows versions with hardware-assisted security features would trigger this check.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBlob Type\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eRaw Byte\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eResolution\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eType 0\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x01\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAllocate Memory -- VirtualAlloc the 53KB execution buffer\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eType 17\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x12\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eException Handler Data -- stores pointer to XOR-encoded API name block used during exception setup\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eType 21\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e0x16\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAnti-Analysis -- conditional abort if hypervisor present + CET Shadow Stack active + OS ≥ Win10 2004\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAppendix C. Detailed Stage 2, infrastructure, and configuration notes\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe assembled payload is a 27KB position-independent shellcode with 74 functions. It implements a full C2 beacon with system profiling, encrypted communication, and remote code execution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNine data collectors run in sequence, each appending results to a session packet:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe process enumeration has a fallback chain. It first tries the Toolhelp API (CreateToolhelp32Snapshot, Process32FirstW/Process32NextW). If that fails, it falls back to NtQuerySystemInformation with information class 5 (SystemProcessInformation), walking the linked list of SYSTEM_PROCESS_INFORMATION structures. Each process name is hashed with MD5 (via the CryptoAPI), and the hash is added to the session packet.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCollected data flows through a multi-stage encryption pipeline before transmission:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe XXTEA implementation uses the standard 32-round Feistel structure with a 128-bit key derived from the configuration. The custom Base64 alphabet changes with each configuration update, which defeats signature-based network detection without adding any complexity to the protocol.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe beacon disguises its check-in as a request for a PNG image:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eGET /assets/static/img/I4o8Pp_41.png HTTP/1.1\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eHost: pick.storewebzone.net\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eUser-Agent: wininet/10.0.19044\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eCache-Control: no-cache\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eConnection: Keep-Alive\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe server's response contains actual PNG data, but with a custom chunk type. The payload scans for the magic byte 0xA1 followed by a 24-byte header:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe encrypted shellcode is decrypted with the second XXTEA key from the configuration, then executed in freshly allocated memory. After execution, the results are XXTEA-encrypted and sent back via HTTP POST to a separate .php endpoint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe payload uses a packet protocol with 22 entry types for serializing collected data:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach entry is prefixed with a type byte and length, forming a TLV (Type-Length-Value) structure that the C2 server parses to reconstruct the victim's profile.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAngrySpark uses two separate C2 channels, each with its own domain, IP address, and encryption layer.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe parent domain server-sys.com was registered in June 2021 through Tucows with WHOIS privacy protection. Several related subdomains (ss1., api., bs.) were observed on the same IP, suggesting purpose-built infrastructure.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth IPs fall within the same 185.151.28.0/22 range, suggesting the operator rented multiple VPS instances from the same UK-based hosting provider.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach configuration update carried new randomized URL paths, though the domain and C2 structure remained consistent:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe pattern is clear: random 6-8 character slug, incrementing 4-digit counter, random filename. Combined with a custom Base64 alphabet that also rotates per update, this defeats static network signatures while maintaining a recognizable structure for the operator.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe VM writes 16 configuration key-value pairs into the payload's data segment:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTwo distinct XXTEA keys serve different purposes: key #1 encrypts outbound data (system profiles), while key #2 decrypts inbound payloads (shellcode from the server). This separation means compromising one key doesn't expose the other direction of communication.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThree memory snapshots, each captured months apart, tell the story of a living operation:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMay 2022: The first variant uses NtQuerySystemInformation(40) (SystemCodeIntegrityInformation). The shellcode prologue differs from later versions. Syscall table has 23 entries, no Insider builds.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJune 2022: Rewritten loader with a different code structure but identical C2 domain. URL paths rotated. Syscall table gains one entry: build 20344 (Win10 Insider Fe/Co), bumping it to 24 entries. The operator was actively updating both code and compatibility data.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJanuary 2023: Same code as June 2022 (identical syscall table), but the NtQuerySystemInformation argument changed to class 10 (SystemModuleInformation) and URLs rotated again. The code was frozen; only the configuration blob changed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy April 2023, the C2 connection was blocked. By May, the beacon domain expired. By June, the DLL's C2 certificate expired. The infrastructure was never renewed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable
