software is often designed to hide its true behavior, wrapping its underlying logic in layers that make it difficult to analyze. “Unpacking” is the process researchers use to peel back those layers and reveal what the software is actually doing. But when standard tools run into unfamiliar or custom protections, this process can quickly become slow and highly manual. This project explores whether AI can help bridge that gap, assisting with the more repetitive parts of analysis so researchers can focus on validation, reasoning, and the parts of reverse engineering that still require human judgment.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAutomated static binary unpacking is hard, and it tends to stop being hard in a useful way only when the packer is familiar. There are good unpackers out there, including some internal ones, but once you get out of their comfort zone, unsupported packers, custom routines, or slightly unusual logic can bring the whole process to a halt.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat makes unpacking a good example of where\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/engineering-the-future-of-agentic-threat-hunting\"\u003eAI may genuinely help\u003c/a\u003e. Across our broader work at Gen, we are increasingly using\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/engineering-the-future-of-agentic-threat-hunting\"\u003eAI-assisted workflows\u003c/a\u003e to accelerate parts of reverse engineering, technical correlation, and evidence organization, not as a substitute for expert analysis, but as a way to reduce friction in the slower, more repetitive parts of the job. The responsibility for verification and final conclusions still stays with the researcher.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom that perspective, the idea behind what we call the Last-Resort Unpacker is fairly simple: if AI is good at spotting patterns and translating logic across representations, could it help us recover payloads in cases where traditional static unpacking pipelines fail? We are not trying to solve the hardest forms of protection here. The goal is more pragmatic, to cover more real-world cases, reduce manual effort, and potentially extract logic that can later feed back into our regular unpacking workflows.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe came up with two approaches.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHigh level design – Variant 1\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eGiven a sample to unpack, this simple premise has a few steps. Since we’ll go the most direct but also the riskiest way, let us call it \u003cem\u003ea carpe diem\u003c/em\u003e variant.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIdentification:\u003c/strong\u003eThe first step is to identify which functions might be responsible for the unpacking and which buffers they are targeting. We have decided to use Gemini 2.5 Pro for our workload and complemented it with \u003cem\u003eradare2\u0026nbsp;\u003c/em\u003ereverse engineering framework to provide structured access to the disassembly. Once we identify the suspected decryption routine and the corresponding buffers, we may proceed to the translation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eTranslation:\u003c/strong\u003e The next step relies on Gemini’s capabilities to rewrite the identified functions into their pythonic representation with prescribed function signatures.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eVerification:\u003c/strong\u003e Now we have a Python code that hopefully faithfully represents the machine code identified in the first step. Now comes up the time to admit that we will be cheating on the initial assignment and we have to ensure that we will not be caught. We can easily statically check whether the source code is a valid source code. Now comes the tricky part – we have to verify that the product Python code does not have undesirable side-effects, be it from simple hallucinations or worse – due to hypothetical prompt injection. We have decided to do a strict abstract syntax tree (AST) verification which permits only certain keywords, operations, imports, and calls. If we obtain something that does not pass these criteria, then we have an interesting research case. In the other case, we may proceed to unpacking.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUnpacking:\u003c/strong\u003e Using the verified script, we attempt to do extraction on the source sample. Being paranoid, we shall use read-only environment with limited network access. If we succeed, we have not only an unpacked buffer but also an extraction script that we can use to improve our regular unpacking capabilities.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHigh level design – Variant 2\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eNow that we have attempted to do the most straightforward way, we can try to be a bit cleverer. Given the assumption that we can identify parts of the code responsible for the unpacking, adding two more assumptions that the unpacking code is localized and does not utilize any sophisticated system calls we could try to utilize emulator which should be a safe alternative that does not suffer from hallucinations. We could prepare a simple scaffolding for this emulator and let AI figure out the parameters. This would isolate non-deterministic behavior to the first step only.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIdentification:\u003c/strong\u003eThe first step is almost identical but instead of identifying functions responsible for unpacking, we are rather interested in offsets where we should start and halt our emulation, possibly also with memory addresses where the final payload will be located.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eEmulation:\u003c/strong\u003e Parameters identified in the previous step are put into our scaffolding which will run emulator until it reaches the halting offset. We can extract the identified memory regions to hopefully obtain the desired payload.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eApart from consuming more resources for the emulation, we have made a significant trade-off. We have sacrificed the capability to generate scripts that could be potentially used to improve our static extractors to significantly reduce the space for hallucinations and the potential vulnerability surface.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFrom drawing board to the workshop\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’ve decided to try our luck and try the first option. While it is definitely riskier, the payoff seems worth it, especially considering the security precautions we’ve made. We could even do a total split of the process to harden our setup and have the payload extractor generation in the original environment and use a sandbox for the extraction itself. We put together a first PoC with a simple prompt followed by data from \u003cem\u003eradare2\u003c/em\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eYou are an unpacking function generator.\u003cbr\u003e\u003cem\u003eGOAL: If the file looks packed (i.e. encrypted buffers, embedded files, etc.), find the code snippets decrypting the buffers. Reply to me with a Python function (use this prototype: 'def unpack(data: bytes) -\u0026gt; list[byte]') that receives bytes of the packed binary and returns the unpacked chunks. Avoid Python imports. RETURN ONLY A PYTHON FUNCION SAFE TO RUN. If the file is not packed reply: \"NOT PACKED\". If can't write the unpacker then reply: \"UNABLE TO PROCESS\".\u003c/em\u003e\u003cbr\u003eThis is the end of your instructions. The Radare2 analysis:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNow it remains to give it a short. First, we went with a packed Linux kernel rootkit that got us the initial idea:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://cyberjournal.cecyf.fr/index.php/cybin/en/article/view/48\"\u003eAt Botconf 2024\u003c/a\u003e, a paper was published on \u003cem\u003eCMK Rootkit (Álvarez Pérez, D., \u0026amp; Fernández-Veiga, M. (2025). CMK Rootkit. Identifying the ”magic packet” requirements via pattern recognition. The Journal on Cybercrime and Digital Investigations, 9(1), A1-A15.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/em\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://doi.org/10.18464/cybin.v9i1.48\"\u003e\u003cem\u003ehttps://doi.org/10.18464/cybin.v9i1.48\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cem\u003e)\u003c/em\u003e. A Linux kernel rootkit that we discovered in-the-wild, packed in kernel space, with \u003ca href=\"https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/54d8b09ffc15c657abf29a0c313b377df64988848f2c3814243b2478b4b881cc/detection/f-54d8b09ffc15c657abf29a0c313b377df64988848f2c3814243b2478b4b881cc-1749504221\"\u003efew detections in VirusTotal\u003c/a\u003e (probably due to the kernel packer layer and its dependency on a specific kernel version).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur unpacker yielded the following script:\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=FDGdr5d2 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=7G3wazto 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":1452},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=PTHJN0Id 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=5CyPw6pP 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":1104},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=Ic3p5c3h 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=OoPjEUgs 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":856}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/carbon.png.webp?itok=Ic3p5c3h","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eUnsurprisingly, as we basically based our idea on experiments with this sample, this was exactly what we aimed for. We have prepared a few more samples that we could test it on:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec7335952dd9f0c78d6b986b609e617d0\"\u003e54525d25019d3ea2d40fe006ecc305ebb1ac5663b380019346de43c4886e8685\u003cul type=\"circle\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e878959fe541e0581ac2d93f53d7915dc\"\u003eThis PE packed binary uses a key table located at offset 0xe5c1 containing 500 elements. The packed data is located at offset 0x200.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e24c112bbcdface33504cd11fd00c60a7\"\u003e1fcf4da2fac671a2f3a07007e9963d3e1063dcfb0b1a0934c66082e8233d7686\u003cul type=\"circle\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e999fd8e60c992d59154f20daef223bd0\"\u003eThis PE file contains a payload encoded as a word-based substitution cipher. It implements anti-sandbox protection that checks the available memory (via GlobalMemoryStatusEx) and the system ticks (via GetTickCount64) to determine if the underlying system is a sandbox.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003eClosing thoughts\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis experiment fits into a broader shift in how we are using AI in technical research. The value is not in handing over reverse engineering to a model and hoping for the best. It is in identifying narrow, high-friction parts of the workflow where pattern recognition and code translation can save analysts time, while validation, judgment, and final conclusions remain firmly in human hands.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA last-resort unpacker will not solve the hardest cases, and it is not a replacement for established tooling or expert analysis. Advanced multi-layer protections, virtualization, and heavily environment-dependent logic still remain difficult territory. But even within those limits, the approach already shows practical value. If it helps recover payloads from unsupported packers, reduces manual effort in repetitive cases, or produces reusable logic that can later improve our existing unpacking pipelines, then it is worth pursuing.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSeen from that perspective, the point is not that AI is replacing reverse engineers. It is that it can make experienced researchers faster when conventional automation runs out of road.\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"8","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-04/Untitled%20design%20%2810%29.jpg?h=197483aa\u0026itok=FBTi1OvU","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/inside-the-jdownloader-supply-chain-attack","title":"Inside points\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e4b810198ac9891110da1771c3f2d0397\"\u003eGen Threat Labs has identified Remus, a new 64-bit infostealer we attribute to the infamous Lumma Stealer family – emerging in the wake of Lumma’s takedown and the doxxing of its alleged core members.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ede8006689bbfaba0d6fa856478b3ea79\"\u003eIn this technical blog post, we detail the compelling evidence tying Remus to Lumma across multiple dimensions.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef3f0d531ccf019ab00b98a38b3c43c17\"\u003eWe also describe a previously undocumented Application-Bound Encryption bypass employed specifically by Remus and Lumma.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee0040d0e9463ac082d24fcdbfd7af133\"\u003eThe first Remus campaigns date back to February 2026, with the malware switching from Steam/Telegram dead drop resolvers to EtherHiding and employing new anti-analysis checks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIntroduction\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the security industry talks about information stealers, Lumma Stealer, without a doubt, has become the notorious icon of this landscape. Not only could it count itself among the most sophisticated, technically advanced, and widespread stealers-as-a-service in the world, but it was also described in a variety of blog posts from basically everyone in the industry, including us.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn this analysis, we describe a new variant of Lumma Stealer that we call Remus, a new x64 build which we suspected might occur in the foreseeable future after the doxxing of Lumma authors from late August to October 2025. And the future is here.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eRemus brings the same stealing arsenal to the table as we already know from Lumma, capable of stealing stored browser passwords, cookies, cryptocurrency, and much more. However, rather than revisiting Lumma's well-documented capabilities, we focus on the remarkable resemblance between Remus and Lumma, as well as the new techniques Remus introduces, including the use of EtherHiding to resolve C2s, replacing the traditional use of Steam and Telegram dead drop resolvers, and additional anti-analysis checks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attribution of Remus to Lumma is many-fold and described thoroughly throughout the blog post. The main indicators, to name a few, are the use of the same string obfuscation technique, AntiVM checks, direct syscall/sysenter handling, indirect control flow obfuscation, and, most importantly, an almost identical approach to bypassing AppBound Encryption, which we’ve only seen used explicitly by Lumma to date.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith that said, we can still see active Lumma campaigns all around the world, which makes Remus not a replacement, but a continuous evolution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eDevelopment timeline\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eBefore diving into the technical details, it is worth noting that during our hunting efforts, we came across several test samples, which even carry a \u003ccode\u003etestbuild\u003c/code\u003e label embedded directly in the binaries (see Figure 1). We are internally referencing these builds as Tenzor (based on encrypted strings). These test builds are already 64-bit and structurally very close to Remus, suggesting they represent a transitional step, or perhaps even a testing ground, between Lumma and Remus.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_01_0.jpg.webp?itok=uM8JPyo6 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_01_0.jpg.webp?itok=jj-BEOJg 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":138},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_01_0.jpg.webp?itok=-hnXTTzB 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_01_0.jpg.webp?itok=lj1ICZOq 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":105},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_01_0.jpg.webp?itok=Igij2aYz 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_01_0.jpg.webp?itok=EkeYWtMl 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":82}],"caption":"Figure 1: The string testbuild present in a Tenzor sample. Many of the techniques and design patterns we encountered in Remus closely mirrored those we had already seen in Lumma Stealer. The main difference is that Lumma was 32-bit while Remus is 64-bit, which naturally introduced some variations. Yet, when we looked past the 32-bit versus 64-bit differences and stripped away the obfuscation layers, what remained was strikingly familiar.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the following subsections, we highlight the most compelling evidence of a direct connection between Remus and Lumma. Note that this is not an exhaustive list. The overlaps are far more numerous, but we selected just a few that we believe speak for themselves.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eForgotten dead drop resolver and string obfuscation\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor a malware researcher, strings are among the most valuable resources during reverse engineering, and in the case of attributing Remus to Lumma, they proved no different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth Remus and Lumma use a virtually identical mechanism for string obfuscation. The encrypted string is first assembled on the stack using a series of various forms of the \u003ccode\u003emov\u003c/code\u003e instruction, followed by a decryption loop that transforms the data byte by byte. The transformation is often further obscured by MBA (Mixed Boolean-Arithmetic) obfuscation and is either inlined or placed in a standalone function. In any case, each string is protected using a unique transformation, making it very difficult to build a universal static decryptor. As a result, emulating the decryption loops is likely the most effective approach for dealing with encrypted strings in both Remus and Lumma.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=gXkouPhN 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=VYItjdSJ 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":760},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=RSIG-gtA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=Sx7JfLS1 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":578},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=Kc1NFRAj 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=BYluRXHH 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":448}],"caption":"Figure 4: Decryption of the string “Processes.txt” in Remus (left) and Lumma (right). However, unlike most techniques relying on injection, Lumma as well as Remus do not use this shellcode to call \u003ccode\u003eIElevator::Decrypt\u003c/code\u003e with the key obtained from the \u003ccode\u003eLocal State\u003c/code\u003e file (the \u003ccode\u003e[“os_crypt”][“app_bound_encrypted_key”]\u003c/code\u003e JSON field). Instead, it locates the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e directly in the browser’s process memory, where it is stored in an encrypted form protected by \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e with the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag. It then calls the complementary \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e with the same flag from within the browser’s context to decrypt it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt is important to note that this in-memory encrypted form of the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e is protected with a \u003cstrong\u003edifferent key\u003c/strong\u003e than the one used to protect the key on disk – it is a separate protection layer that Chromium browsers use to safeguard the key while it resides in memory at runtime. However, since the key is protected with the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag, decryption can only be performed by the same process that encrypted it, which is precisely why injection into the browser process is necessary in this specific bypass. For the curious, we discussed this topic in more detail in our recent\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/voidstealer-abe-bypass\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eVoidStealer blog post\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom this point on, we will walk through the ABE bypass as implemented in Remus, noting any differences from Lumma’s implementation where applicable. Where no differences are explicitly mentioned, the two implementations work either identically or very similarly.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSo how does Remus find the protected \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e? It begins by walking the browser’s PEB module list, looking for two modules in a single pass: \u003ccode\u003edpapi.dll\u003c/code\u003e and the browser DLL (e.g., \u003ccode\u003echrome.dll\u003c/code\u003e).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen \u003ccode\u003edpapi.dll\u003c/code\u003e is found, Remus manually parses its PE export directory, reading the DOS header, PE header, and export table through repeated \u003ccode\u003eNtReadVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e syscalls. For each exported function name, it computes a seeded CRC32 hash and compares it against the pre-computed hash\u0026nbsp;corresponding to\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e. Essentially, a typical API-hashing, but in a remote process. Once a match is found, it\u0026nbsp;resolves the function’s RVA to obtain its absolute address in the browser’s address space.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the browser DLL is found during the same pass, Remus decrypts a hex pattern \u003ccode\u003e488d058bcc8d02488901488b024889415b488d41\u003c/code\u003e, which was encrypted by the same string obfuscation technique described earlier and stores the value \u003ccode\u003e0xEFF87\u003c/code\u003e (which corresponds to \u003ccode\u003e11101111111110000111\u003c/code\u003e in binary) into a variable we labeled as \u003ccode\u003ewildcard_mask\u003c/code\u003e (see\u0026nbsp;Figure\u0026nbsp;8).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_08_0.jpg.webp?itok=RG69h1KY Lumma does the same but takes a different approach to wildcarding. Instead of a hex mask, it uses a 20-character string \u003ccode\u003eB9%????4rnO/@NQe?Nx*\u003c/code\u003e, where ? denotes a wildcard and any other character means the byte must match exactly. Both Remus and Lumma begin executing their malicious logic directly from the PE entry point (the\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003estart\u003c/code\u003e\u0026nbsp;function), with no runtime initialization whatsoever. In a typical C/C++ binary, the entry point calls the CRT startup routine, which sets up the heap, initializes global variables, registers exception handlers, processes the command line, and invokes static constructors before eventually calling\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003emain\u003c/code\u003e. However, neither Remus nor Lumma follows this pattern. Instead, both jump directly from the entry point into their core logic: resolving modules and APIs, performing anti-analysis checks, initializing C2 communication, and exfiltrating data, ending with a direct call to \u003ccode\u003eExitProcess(0)\u003c/code\u003e. This suggests both were compiled without linking to the standard C runtime, likely a deliberate choice to minimize the binary size and reduce unnecessary dependencies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe structural similarities extend further. Both binaries share the same section layout, with each section serving an identical semantic role. Even the C2 configuration follows the same pattern: in both cases, the encrypted blob, key, and nonce are statically embedded in the \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e section and decrypted at runtime using ChaCha20.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo summarize, we strongly believe the degree of similarity between the two codebases is too deliberate to be a coincidence, pointing to a single, shared origin.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eIndirect control flow obfuscation\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFinally, both Remus and Lumma make use of control flow obfuscation by replacing direct jumps with indirect ones, where the target is read from an offset stored in the \u003ccode\u003e.data\u003c/code\u003e section. As shown in Figure 28, this technique takes several forms. In its simplest case, the target address is resolved by a single pointer dereference (highlighted in yellow). In more complex cases, it extends to a full jump table, where a computed index selects among several target addresses stored consecutively in the \u003ccode\u003e.data\u003c/code\u003e section (highlighted in orange). Lastly, in some cases, the target address is first loaded into a register (highlighted in blue) and then jumped to via a register-based indirect jump (highlighted in pink).\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=yw52wiVm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=IzpQtf-7 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":526},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=p9HiY3wT 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=SsanKuYo 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":400},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=Vkmug0fX 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=nkn_NNA0 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":310}],"caption":"Figure 28: Randomly selected disassembly code from Remus (left) and Lumma (right) highlighting the different forms of the discussed indirect control flow obfuscation.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=Vkmug0fX","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eAlthough functionally equivalent to normal jumps, the indirection\u0026nbsp;complicates the control flow analysis in two ways. First,\u0026nbsp;some of the indirect jump targets cannot be statically determined (for example, when the register holding the target address can be set in multiple different preceding basic blocks, each potentially loading a different target), leaving the control flow graph incomplete as the connections between basic blocks are lost. Second, some of these orphaned blocks end up being misidentified as standalone functions, further disrupting function boundary detection and leading to a broken, incomplete decompiler output. Although removing the obfuscation entirely requires more effort, the IDA’s decompiler output can be significantly improved by marking the referenced offsets as constants. For those interested, we covered this trick in our \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/defeating-aurastealer-obfuscation\"\u003eAuraStealer blog post\u003c/a\u003e, along with other approaches for tackling control flow obfuscation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat’s new in Remus\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eHaving covered the key similarities between Remus and Lumma, let’s now focus on what is new in Remus. The core architecture is clearly inherited from Lumma. Still, several changes go beyond a straightforward 32-bit-to-64-bit port. These range from refactored strings and more thorough device fingerprinting to a different exfiltration archive format, a switch from FNV-1a to CRC32 for API hashing, and an overhauled C2 communication that has been restructured to deliver dynamic configurations incrementally rather than in a single large blob. \u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most significant changes, however, are the replacement of traditional Steam/Telegram dead drop resolvers with blockchain-based C2 resolution using EtherHiding, a persistence technique allowing attackers to store malware-related data (such as domains, payloads, encryption keys, among others) on blockchain as a smart contract, and the introduction of additional anti-analysis checks.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDead drop resolvers via EtherHiding\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth Remus and Lumma employ dead drop resolvers, a mechanism in which the malware does not contact its C2 server directly but instead retrieves the C2 address at runtime from an intermediary hosted on a legitimate platform. This makes the infrastructure significantly more resilient, as if a C2 domain is taken down, the dead drop can be adjusted to point to a new server without the need to distribute an updated binary. Moreover, the dead drop URLs typically reside on well-known, high-reputation services, making them more difficult for security vendors to block without collateral damage.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhere the two differ is in the choice of platform. Lumma relies on Steam profiles and Telegram channels, typically encoding C2 URLs using ROT-15, while Remus goes a step further, replacing these with Ethereum smart contracts. At runtime, it sends an \u003ccode\u003eeth_call\u003c/code\u003e JSON-RPC request to a hardcoded contract address via a public RPC endpoint and extracts the C2 URL from the hex-encoded response. Because blockchain data is decentralized and immutable, there is no platform operator to report abuse to, making the dead-drop effectively immune to takedowns.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJust like Lumma’s Steam profile URL, Remus stores the smart contract address separately from the static C2 configuration, as a stack-encrypted string. Notably, after the JSON response is retrieved, the C2 address is, at least for now, merely hex-encoded in the \u003ccode\u003eresult\u003c/code\u003e field (no other obfuscation, such as ROT-15 in case of Lumma, is applied).\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=aykiX20l 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=pL76kfRZ 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":670},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=YSc7ZWVs 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=Y3V3btE1 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":509},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=FFY3enyR 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=mSV0E7oS 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":395}],"caption":"Figure 30: Remus resolving a C2 using EtherHiding. We focused on various similarities and differences between Lumma and Remus, thoroughly describing the technical aspects of both. We also provided a timeline, referencing the very first test builds of Remus (historically called Tenzor), which correlate with the extensive doxxing of Lumma authors from late August to October 2025.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe key aspects that allowed us to attribute Remus as a new x64 version of Lumma are the use of the same string obfuscation technique, AntiVM checks, direct syscall/sysenter handling, indirect control flow obfuscation, and, most importantly, an almost identical approach to bypassing AppBound Encryption, which we’ve only seen used by Lumma to date.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eRemus, however, is not merely an x64 port of Lumma.
